|**7a. What do you think of the current treatments and**<br>**care available for EGFR mutation-positive advanced**<br>**non-small-cell lung cancer on the NHS?**<br>**7b. How do your views on these current treatments**<br>**compare to those of other people that you may be**<br>**aware of?**|I am only am only aware of the treatments my wife received and they are, in my<br>view, a two-edged sword. On diagnosis, she was given a few months to live, but<br>after spinal surgery and the targeted therapies of first gefitinib and then osimertinib,<br>she lived for 2 years and 8 months after diagnosis; far longer than her doctors<br>expected.<br>The ease by which the drugs were taken – merely a tablet every day- was<br>extremely helpful and meant we did not need visit to hospital as often.<br>The gefitinib shrank her tumours, but stopped being effective after 18 months. The<br>osimertinib was effective for perhaps 8 months. While the tumours were shrinking,<br>she was able to self-care a little more – perhaps heating some soup, or washing<br>herself. She was unable to use a bath but could use the shower in the garden room<br>we had built. This became increasingly more difficult as the drugs started to fail and<br>the tumours started to grow and spread. But she was never able to return to<br>anything remotely like her previous life of going to work, gardening, going out with<br>friends and so on.<br>As there is so little hope for people diagnosed with later stage NSCLC, the fact that<br>there are drug treatments at all is incredibly important. I remember the elation we<br>felt when we discovered that my wife was genetically compatible with the therapies<br>she was given. However, these treatments should in my view be given alongside<br>advocacy, counselling and psychological support, as well as honest and detailed<br>information about management of side-effects and drug efficacy for this incredibly<br>vulnerable group of patients. In my view, the terminally ill are the most vulnerable<br>group of patients the NHS cares for. Their needs, especially their psychological<br>needs, are so often left unmet.<br>It was clearly positive that she lived to see our son’s sixteenth and seventeenth<br>birthdays, and witnessed him getting into college to study science. Precious<br>moments we will treasure. We managed to go away on holiday twice, though by the<br>second holiday her agoraphobia and diarrhoea were so severe, so never left the<br>cottage.|
|---|---|



Patient expert statement 

Osimertinib with pemetrexed and platinum-based chemotherapy for untreated EGFR mutation-positive advanced non-small-cell lung cancer [ID6328] 6 of 11

|**8. If there are disadvantages for patients of current**<br>**NHS treatments for EGFR mutation-positive advanced**<br>**non-small-cell lung cancer (for example, how they are**<br>**given or taken, side effects of treatment, and any**<br>**others) please describe these**|Though the drug therapies extended my wife’s life expectancy, her quality of life<br>was increasingly poor, though I suspect this was mostly as a result of her condition,<br>not side-effects from the drugs. However, the side effects were sometimes difficult:<br>severe diarrhoea at times being the worst, but loss of appetite too. Itching and a<br>skin rash affected her but her precarious mental health meant she used the<br>medication as an excuse to avoid sunlight and stay indoors. She seemed, for<br>example, to fixate on various skincare products which she believed were unsafe to<br>use while taking the medication, when I’m not sure there is much evidence for that. I<br>often felt it might cheer her up if she had a small gin as she had enjoyed a ‘drink’ in<br>happier times, though I was not able to convince her to do that, despite<br>reassurances from her clinicians and I suspect that distorted concerns over the side<br>effects of the drugs were a factor in this. Indeed, I sometimes found it difficult to be<br>able to distinguish between genuine and perceived side-effects because her mental<br>health was so precarious. She received little if no meaningful psychological care<br>which might have made her final few years easier and perhaps derive more benefit<br>from the medication.<br>Both the gefitinib and osimertinib were prescribed after hospital visits, entailing long<br>waits at our hospital pharmacy, which were exhausting for both me and my wife.<br>However, we discovered we could wait for the drugs to be dispensed at a Maggies<br>centre nearby, a wonderfully welcoming place and so unlike anything we’d seen<br>before in the health system.<br>It would be helpful for sick and terminally ill patients to be prioritised in pharmacies<br>so they do not have to wait too long, or at least can wait in comfort. She found it<br>easy to swallow the tablets, but was increasingly confused about when and which<br>tablets to take. She took at least 30 pills day, so explanation and support on taking<br>any medication is essential.|
|---|---|
|**9a. If there are advantages of osimertinib with**<br>**pemetrexed and platinum-based chemotherapy over**<br>**current treatments on the NHS please describe these.**|The reassurance that this combination will extend a patient’s life by a significant<br>period of time, compared to osimertinib alone, will clearly be important for many<br>patients. It seems to me that this regime could be extremely debilitating for many<br>patients so they would need to be given full information before deciding to go<br>ahead.|
